{"candidates": ["Major Depressive Disorder with Psychotic Features", "Delusional Disorder"], "note": "The patient's description of feeling down and depressed, along with a lack of interest in previously enjoyed activities, initially supports the continued consideration of Major Depressive Disorder with Psychotic Features. However, the absence of reported hallucinations (hearing or seeing things that aren't there) and the presence of a fixed, unshakable belief in being responsible for terrible events (a delusion of guilt), without mention of disorganized thinking or other psychotic symptoms typical of schizophrenia, introduces Delusional Disorder as a strong candidate. The patient's delusional belief is non-bizarre (involves situations that occur in real life, such as feeling responsible for disasters) and has been present for two months, which aligns with the criteria for Delusional Disorder. Obsessive-Compulsive Disorder is less likely given the patient's description of their beliefs as delusional rather than obsessive thoughts, and the lack of compulsions to perform specific rituals or behaviors. Therefore, Delusional Disorder is added as a candidate, while Obsessive-Compulsive Disorder is removed due to the nature of the patient's beliefs and behaviors not aligning closely with the criteria for OCD.", "is_final": false}

I just know it is entirely my fault, no matter what anyone else says.\n\nDoctor: How have these beliefs and feelings of responsibility affected your daily life and relationships with others over the past two months?\n\nPatient: These feelings of guilt have been going on for the last two months, and they have completely taken over my life. I've stopped talking to my friends and family because I feel like a massive burden to them. I also have absolutely no motivation left to do my job or handle any of my daily tasks.\n\nDoctor: Are your feelings of depression, such as loss of interest and motivation, secondary to your delusional beliefs of being responsible for terrible things, or do you experience depressive symptoms independently of these beliefs?"
  }

I'm really not sure how they are connected or which one started first. All I know is that I feel a profound sense of guilt and, at the same time, a complete lack of interest and motivation. Both of these feelings are just constantly there and feel very overwhelming.

But I don't do any physical rituals or specific behaviors to try and prevent them.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these thoughts about being responsible for terrible things are definitely true, or do you have some doubt about their validity, even if the doubt is small?\n\nPatient: There is no doubt in my mind at all. I am absolutely certain that I am the one responsible for these terrible things happening. I just know it is entirely my fault, no matter what anyone else says.\n\nDoctor: How have these beliefs and feelings of responsibility affected your daily life and relationships with others over the past two months?\n\nPatient: These feelings of guilt have been going on for the last two months, and they have completely taken over my life. I've stopped talking to my friends and family because I feel like a massive burden to them. I also have absolutely no motivation left to do my job or handle any of my daily tasks.\n\nDoctor: Are your feelings of depression, such as loss of interest and motivation, secondary to your delusional beliefs of being responsible for terrible things, or do you experience depressive symptoms independently of these beliefs?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure how they are connected or which one started first. All I know is that I feel a profound sense of guilt and, at the same time, a complete lack of interest and motivation. Both of these feelings are just constantly there and feel very overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about when these delusional beliefs and depressive symptoms started, and did one seem to precede the other or did they start around the same time?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Both the intense guilt and my lack of interest have been going on for about two months now. I'm really not sure if one started before the other, or if they both began at the exact same time. It all just seemed to happen together and has been constant since then.

Everyone tells me it's impossible for me to have caused them, but I just can't shake the belief that it's my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about these beliefs of being responsible for terrible things, and do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors to try to alleviate these thoughts or prevent these events from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, I constantly dwell on these thoughts and keep blaming myself for all these terrible things. I'm really not sure if one started before the other, or if they both began at the exact same time. It all just seemed to happen together and has been constant since then.\n\nDoctor: Do your delusional beliefs of being responsible for terrible things dominate your thoughts and emotions most of the time, or are they more of a component of your overall depressive state?"

Both of these feelings are just constantly there and feel very overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about when these delusional beliefs and depressive symptoms started, and did one seem to precede the other or did they start around the same time?\n\nPatient: Both the intense guilt and my lack of interest have been going on for about two months now. I'm really not sure if one started before the other, or if they both began at the exact same time. It all just seemed to happen together and has been constant since then.\n\nDoctor: Do your delusional beliefs of being responsible for terrible things dominate your thoughts and emotions most of the time, or are they more of a component of your overall depressive state?\n\nPatient: I would say they are a heavy, constant component of my overall depressed state. For the past two months, this deep feeling of being responsible for terrible things has just been a major part of how down and empty I feel every day.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other delusional beliefs you have besides the one about being responsible for terrible things, or is this the only fixed belief that you've been experiencing?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any other beliefs like that. This overwhelming feeling of responsibility and guilt for all the terrible things happening is the only one, but it is constantly weighing on me."
  }
]

Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger.
